leaves seat in situations when remaining seated is expected.\n  manifestation: When I’m supposed to sit through a training, dinner with the family, or a school meeting, I keep finding reasons to get up—refill water, check the copier, look in on my relative—even when I know it makes me look disrespectful.\n- key: S013\n  name: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\n  description: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n  manifestation: Quiet time at home has become almost impossible for me, because if my spouse is trying to watch a show or my relative is resting, I’ll start narrating what I’m doing, humming, or moving things around until someone asks me to please settle down.\n- key: S014\n  name: Constant_Activity\n  description: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: What brings me in is that I feel like I’m constantly running on a motor lately, like I finish one thing at school or home and immediately jump into the next without ever being able to shut myself off.\n- key: S015\n  name: Excessive_Talking\n  description: Often talks excessively.\n  manifestation: I’ll go into a parent conference or a staff meeting meaning to be brief, and then I hear myself giving three examples, a story from my classroom, and ideas nobody asked for while the other person is clearly trying to wrap it up.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: I keep jumping into conversations between colleagues, parents, or my spouse and our relative because I think I’m helping, but I end up talking over them or taking charge before they’ve even had a chance to finish.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: My mother used to joke that even in elementary school I taught the class from What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I’m here because lately I feel like I’m just… always going. Like I finish one thing at school or at home and before I even take a breath, I’m already jumping into the next thing. It’s like I can’t quite shut myself off, if that makes sense. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s been hard to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think I do, though I don’t mean to be rude or anything. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I’m here because lately I feel like I’m just… always going. Like I finish one thing at school or at home and before I even take a breath, I’m already jumping into the next thing. It’s like I can’t quite shut myself off, if that makes sense. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s been hard to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think I do, though I don’t mean to be rude or anything. It’s more like I jump in because I think I’m helping. At school, with colleagues or parents, I’ll sort of take over the conversation before they’ve finished. And at home too, with my wife or relatives, I’ll start talking over them because I feel like I know where it’s going.\n\nI realize afterward that I didn’t really give them a chance to finish. I’m not trying to bulldoze people, but… yeah, I guess that is me acting before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to focus on one thing for a long time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I can focus when I need to, especially if it’s something important. Maybe I get a little restless with longer things, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s a major problem. I mean, I try to keep it contained, especially in professional settings, but in IEP meetings or my admin classes my leg will be bouncing under the table, or I’ll be clicking a pen, shifting around in the chair.\n\nSometimes I don’t even notice I’m doing it until someone glances over, and then I feel a little embarrassed. I’m not trying to be disruptive, obviously, but sitting still for long stretches is… hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have mood swings?\n\nPatient: Mood swings… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have big mood swings, at least not that I’ve noticed. I mean, I can get frustrated or keyed up like anyone, but I try not to take that out on people.\n\nMaybe my wife would have a better read on that than I do. But from my perspective, I don’t think it’s a major thing. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I mean… probably, yes. I don’t like to think of myself as dominating a room, but I can definitely talk more than I intend to.\n\nLike in a parent conference or a staff meeting, I’ll go in meaning to keep it short, and then I’ll hear myself giving three examples, or telling a classroom story, or offering ideas that… maybe nobody actually asked for. And then I’ll notice the other person kind of trying to wrap it up, and I feel bad. I’m not trying to be rude or anything. I just get going sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I try pretty hard to stay organized, especially with work, because I kind of have to.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m naturally organized, exactly, but I’ve built routines and lists and reminders so things don’t fall through the cracks. So… difficulty organizing? Maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than what most people deal with. Does that make sense, or are you asking more about planning steps and keeping track of materials?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I probably do, more than I’d like to admit.

I don’t mean to cut people off, but I’ll think I know where they’re going, or I’ll think I’m helping, and I jump in. It happens with colleagues, sometimes with parents at school, and definitely at home with my wife or relatives. I’ll start answering or sort of taking charge before they’ve even finished what they were saying.

I usually realize it afterward—or when I see their face—and then I feel bad, because I’m not trying to be disrespectful. I just… jump in too fast sometimes.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… yes, though I don’t like the word “intrude,” if that makes sense.

It’s more like I’ll jump into a conversation between colleagues, or with parents, or at home with my wife and a relative, because I think I’m being helpful or moving things along. But then I realize I’ve kind of talked over people or taken charge before they even finished saying what they needed to say.

I’m not trying to be rude. I usually mean well. But yes, I can see how it comes across that way sometimes.

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I wish I could say it’s just a personality thing or that I’m just enthusiastic, but it is causing problems.

At work, it’s gotten more serious than I’m comfortable admitting. There have been enough complaints that I was put on a corrective plan, which is… embarrassing. I’m not trying to be difficult—I really do think I’m trying to help or keep things moving—but I can see that people don’t always experience it that way.

And at home, my wife has said that caregiving routines are harder because everyone has to manage my energy too, on top of everything else. That’s hard to hear. I feel like I’m damaging trust with colleagues and with my family, even though my intention is usually to help. Does that make sense?

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I’m here because lately I feel like I’m just… always going. Like I finish one thing at school or at home and before I even take a breath, I’m already jumping into the next thing. It’s like I can’t quite shut myself off, if that makes sense. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s been hard to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think I do, though I don’t mean to be rude or anything. It’s more like I jump in because I think I’m helping. At school, with colleagues or parents, I’ll sort of take over the conversation before they’ve finished. And at home too, with my wife or relatives, I’ll start talking over them because I feel like I know where it’s going.\n\nI realize afterward that I didn’t really give them a chance to finish. I’m not trying to bulldoze people, but… yeah, I guess that is me acting before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to focus on one thing for a long time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I can focus when I need to, especially if it’s something important. Maybe I get a little restless with longer things, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s a major problem. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fidgety or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, more than I probably like to admit. I mean, I try to keep it contained, especially in professional settings, but in IEP meetings or my admin classes my leg will be bouncing under the table, or I’ll be clicking a pen, shifting around in the chair.\n\nSometimes I don’t even notice I’m doing it until someone glances over, and then I feel a little embarrassed. I’m not trying to be disruptive, obviously, but sitting still for long stretches is… hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have mood swings?\n\nPatient: Mood swings… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have big mood swings, at least not that I’ve noticed. I mean, I can get frustrated or keyed up like anyone, but I try not to take that out on people.\n\nMaybe my wife would have a better read on that than I do. But from my perspective, I don’t think it’s a major thing. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I mean… probably, yes. I don’t like to think of myself as dominating a room, but I can definitely talk more than I intend to.\n\nLike in a parent conference or a staff meeting, I’ll go in meaning to keep it short, and then I’ll hear myself giving three examples, or telling a classroom story, or offering ideas that… maybe nobody actually asked for. And then I’ll notice the other person kind of trying to wrap it up, and I feel bad. I’m not trying to be rude or anything. I just get going sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I try pretty hard to stay organized, especially with work, because I kind of have to.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m naturally organized, exactly, but I’ve built routines and lists and reminders so things don’t fall through the cracks. So… difficulty organizing? Maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than what most people deal with. Does that make sense, or are you asking more about planning steps and keeping track of materials?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I probably do, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI don’t mean to cut people off, but I’ll think I know where they’re going, or I’ll think I’m helping, and I jump in. It happens with colleagues, sometimes with parents at school, and definitely at home with my wife or relatives. I’ll start answering or sort of taking charge before they’ve even finished what they were saying.\n\nI usually realize it afterward—or when I see their face—and then I feel bad, because I’m not trying to be disrespectful. I just… jump in too fast sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often fidget or feel restless when you're alone or in quiet environments?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m not running around or anything, but even when things are quiet, I don’t seem to sit still very well.\n\nLike in IEP meetings or in my admin classes, I’ll realize my leg has been bouncing under the table the whole time, or I’m clicking a pen, or shifting around in the chair. Sometimes people glance over, and that’s when I notice I’m doing it. It’s a little embarrassing, honestly, because I’m not trying to be distracting. It just kind of happens before I realize it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often intrude on others or insert yourself into their activities without being asked?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, though I don’t like the word “intrude,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll jump into a conversation between colleagues, or with parents, or at home with my wife and a relative, because I think I’m being helpful or moving things along. But then I realize I’ve kind of talked over people or taken charge before they even finished saying what they needed to say.\n\nI’m not trying to be rude. I usually mean well. But yes, I can see how it comes across that way sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behaviors or tendencies are causing problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wish I could say it’s just a personality thing or that I’m just enthusiastic, but it is causing problems.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten more serious than I’m comfortable admitting.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound like I was some terrible kid or anything, but this goes way back.

My mother used to joke that in elementary school I was “teaching the class from my seat,” because I’d be out of my chair, talking, or cutting in before the teacher had even finished giving directions. She said it with humor, but… looking back, it does sound like the same pattern.

Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fidgety or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, more than I probably like to admit. I mean, I try to keep it contained, especially in professional settings, but in IEP meetings or my admin classes my leg will be bouncing under the table, or I’ll be clicking a pen, shifting around in the chair.\n\nSometimes I don’t even notice I’m doing it until someone glances over, and then I feel a little embarrassed. I’m not trying to be disruptive, obviously, but sitting still for long stretches is… hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have mood swings?\n\nPatient: Mood swings… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have big mood swings, at least not that I’ve noticed. I mean, I can get frustrated or keyed up like anyone, but I try not to take that out on people.\n\nMaybe my wife would have a better read on that than I do. But from my perspective, I don’t think it’s a major thing. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I mean… probably, yes. I don’t like to think of myself as dominating a room, but I can definitely talk more than I intend to.\n\nLike in a parent conference or a staff meeting, I’ll go in meaning to keep it short, and then I’ll hear myself giving three examples, or telling a classroom story, or offering ideas that… maybe nobody actually asked for. And then I’ll notice the other person kind of trying to wrap it up, and I feel bad. I’m not trying to be rude or anything. I just get going sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I try pretty hard to stay organized, especially with work, because I kind of have to.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m naturally organized, exactly, but I’ve built routines and lists and reminders so things don’t fall through the cracks. So… difficulty organizing? Maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than what most people deal with. Does that make sense, or are you asking more about planning steps and keeping track of materials?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I probably do, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI don’t mean to cut people off, but I’ll think I know where they’re going, or I’ll think I’m helping, and I jump in. But yes, I can see how it comes across that way sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behaviors or tendencies are causing problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wish I could say it’s just a personality thing or that I’m just enthusiastic, but it is causing problems.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten more serious than I’m comfortable admitting.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I’m here because lately I feel like I’m just… always going. Like I finish one thing at school or at home and before I even take a breath, I’m already jumping into the next thing. It’s like I can’t quite shut myself off, if that makes sense. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s been hard to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think I do, though I don’t mean to be rude or anything. It’s more like I jump in because I think I’m helping. At school, with colleagues or parents, I’ll sort of take over the conversation before they’ve finished. And at home too, with my wife or relatives, I’ll start talking over them because I feel like I know where it’s going.\n\nI realize afterward that I didn’t really give them a chance to finish. I’m not trying to bulldoze people, but… yeah, I guess that is me acting before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to focus on one thing for a long time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I can focus when I need to, especially if it’s something important. Maybe I get a little restless with longer things, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s a major problem. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fidgety or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, more than I probably like to admit. I mean, I try to keep it contained, especially in professional settings, but in IEP meetings or my admin classes my leg will be bouncing under the table, or I’ll be clicking a pen, shifting around in the chair.\n\nSometimes I don’t even notice I’m doing it until someone glances over, and then I feel a little embarrassed. I’m not trying to be disruptive, obviously, but sitting still for long stretches is… hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have mood swings?\n\nPatient: Mood swings… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have big mood swings, at least not that I’ve noticed. I mean, I can get frustrated or keyed up like anyone, but I try not to take that out on people.\n\nMaybe my wife would have a better read on that than I do. But from my perspective, I don’t think it’s a major thing. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I mean… probably, yes. I don’t like to think of myself as dominating a room, but I can definitely talk more than I intend to.\n\nLike in a parent conference or a staff meeting, I’ll go in meaning to keep it short, and then I’ll hear myself giving three examples, or telling a classroom story, or offering ideas that… maybe nobody actually asked for. And then I’ll notice the other person kind of trying to wrap it up, and I feel bad. I’m not trying to be rude or anything. I just get going sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I try pretty hard to stay organized, especially with work, because I kind of have to.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m naturally organized, exactly, but I’ve built routines and lists and reminders so things don’t fall through the cracks. So… difficulty organizing? Maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than what most people deal with. Does that make sense, or are you asking more about planning steps and keeping track of materials?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I probably do, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI don’t mean to cut people off, but I’ll think I know where they’re going, or I’ll think I’m helping, and I jump in. It happens with colleagues, sometimes with parents at school, and definitely at home with my wife or relatives. I’ll start answering or sort of taking charge before they’ve even finished what they were saying.\n\nI usually realize it afterward—or when I see their face—and then I feel bad, because I’m not trying to be disrespectful. I just… jump in too fast sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often fidget or feel restless when you're alone or in quiet environments?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m not running around or anything, but even when things are quiet, I don’t seem to sit still very well.\n\nLike in IEP meetings or in my admin classes, I’ll realize my leg has been bouncing under the table the whole time, or I’m clicking a pen, or shifting around in the chair. Sometimes people glance over, and that’s when I notice I’m doing it. It’s a little embarrassing, honestly, because I’m not trying to be distracting. It just kind of happens before I realize it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often intrude on others or insert yourself into their activities without being asked?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, though I don’t like the word “intrude,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll jump into a conversation between colleagues, or with parents, or at home with my wife and a relative, because I think I’m being helpful or moving things along. But then I realize I’ve kind of talked over people or taken charge before they even finished saying what they needed to say.\n\nI’m not trying to be rude. I usually mean well. But yes, I can see how it comes across that way sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behaviors or tendencies are causing problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wish I could say it’s just a personality thing or that I’m just enthusiastic, but it is causing problems.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten more serious than I’m comfortable admitting. There have been enough complaints that I was put on a corrective plan, which is… embarrassing. I’m not trying to be difficult—I really do think I’m trying to help or keep things moving—but I can see that people don’t always experience it that way.\n\nAnd at home, my wife has said that caregiving routines are harder because everyone has to manage my energy too, on top of everything else. That’s hard to hear. I feel like I’m damaging trust with colleagues and with my family, even though my intention is usually to help. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms for most of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound like I was some terrible kid or anything, but this goes way back.\n\nMy mother used to joke that in elementary school I was “teaching the class from my seat,” because I’d be out of my chair, talking, or cutting in before the teacher had even finished giving directions. She said it with humor, but… looking back, it does sound like the same pattern.\n\nSo no, I don’t think this just started recently with the family situation or stress. It’s been part of me for a long time. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often dominate conversations with friends or family members outside of work or school?\n\nPatient: Yes… probably more than I realize. I don’t mean to dominate, exactly, but I can see that I do.\n\nEven outside of work, I’ll start out trying to make a quick point, and then suddenly I’m giving three examples, telling some related story, and offering ideas nobody really asked for. I can sometimes notice the other person is kind of trying to wrap it up, and I’m still going.\n\nI don’t want to sound like I’m just talking at people all the time, but… yes, I think that happens.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I’m here because lately I feel like I’m just… always going. Like I finish one thing at school or at home and before I even take a breath, I’m already jumping into the next thing. It’s like I can’t quite shut myself off, if that makes sense. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s been hard to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think I do, though I don’t mean to be rude or anything. It’s more like I jump in because I think I’m helping. At school, with colleagues or parents, I’ll sort of take over the conversation before they’ve finished. And at home too, with my wife or relatives, I’ll start talking over them because I feel like I know where it’s going.\n\nI realize afterward that I didn’t really give them a chance to finish. I’m not trying to bulldoze people, but… yeah, I guess that is me acting before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to focus on one thing for a long time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I can focus when I need to, especially if it’s something important. Maybe I get a little restless with longer things, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s a major problem. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fidgety or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, more than I probably like to admit. I mean, I try to keep it contained, especially in professional settings, but in IEP meetings or my admin classes my leg will be bouncing under the table, or I’ll be clicking a pen, shifting around in the chair.\n\nSometimes I don’t even notice I’m doing it until someone glances over, and then I feel a little embarrassed. I’m not trying to be disruptive, obviously, but sitting still for long stretches is… hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have mood swings?\n\nPatient: Mood swings… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have big mood swings, at least not that I’ve noticed. I mean, I can get frustrated or keyed up like anyone, but I try not to take that out on people.\n\nMaybe my wife would have a better read on that than I do. But from my perspective, I don’t think it’s a major thing. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I mean… probably, yes. I don’t like to think of myself as dominating a room, but I can definitely talk more than I intend to.\n\nLike in a parent conference or a staff meeting, I’ll go in meaning to keep it short, and then I’ll hear myself giving three examples, or telling a classroom story, or offering ideas that… maybe nobody actually asked for. And then I’ll notice the other person kind of trying to wrap it up, and I feel bad. I’m not trying to be rude or anything. I just get going sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I try pretty hard to stay organized, especially with work, because I kind of have to.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m naturally organized, exactly, but I’ve built routines and lists and reminders so things don’t fall through the cracks. So… difficulty organizing? Maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than what most people deal with. Does that make sense, or are you asking more about planning steps and keeping track of materials?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I probably do, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI don’t mean to cut people off, but I’ll think I know where they’re going, or I’ll think I’m helping, and I jump in. It happens with colleagues, sometimes with parents at school, and definitely at home with my wife or relatives. I’ll start answering or sort of taking charge before they’ve even finished what they were saying.\n\nI usually realize it afterward—or when I see their face—and then I feel bad, because I’m not trying to be disrespectful. I just… jump in too fast sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often fidget or feel restless when you're alone or in quiet environments?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m not running around or anything, but even when things are quiet, I don’t seem to sit still very well.\n\nLike in IEP meetings or in my admin classes, I’ll realize my leg has been bouncing under the table the whole time, or I’m clicking a pen, or shifting around in the chair. Sometimes people glance over, and that’s when I notice I’m doing it. It’s a little embarrassing, honestly, because I’m not trying to be distracting. It just kind of happens before I realize it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often intrude on others or insert yourself into their activities without being asked?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, though I don’t like the word “intrude,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll jump into a conversation between colleagues, or with parents, or at home with my wife and a relative, because I think I’m being helpful or moving things along. But then I realize I’ve kind of talked over people or taken charge before they even finished saying what they needed to say.\n\nI’m not trying to be rude. I usually mean well. But yes, I can see how it comes across that way sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behaviors or tendencies are causing problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wish I could say it’s just a personality thing or that I’m just enthusiastic, but it is causing problems.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten more serious than I’m comfortable admitting. There have been enough complaints that I was put on a corrective plan, which is… embarrassing. I’m not trying to be difficult—I really do think I’m trying to help or keep things moving—but I can see that people don’t always experience it that way.\n\nAnd at home, my wife has said that caregiving routines are harder because everyone has to manage my energy too, on top of everything else. That’s hard to hear. I feel like I’m damaging trust with colleagues and with my family, even though my intention is usually to help. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms for most of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound like I was some terrible kid or anything, but this goes way back.\n\nMy mother used to joke that in elementary school I was “teaching the class from my seat,” because I’d be out of my chair, talking, or cutting in before the teacher had even finished giving directions. She said it with humor, but… looking back, it does sound like the same pattern.\n\nSo no, I don’t think this just started recently with the family situation or stress. It’s been part of me for a long time. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often dominate conversations with friends or family members outside of work or school?\n\nPatient: Yes… probably more than I realize. I don’t mean to dominate, exactly, but I can see that I do.\n\nEven outside of work, I’ll start out trying to make a quick point, and then suddenly I’m giving three examples, telling some related story, and offering ideas nobody really asked for. I can sometimes notice the other person is kind of trying to wrap it up, and I’m still going.\n\nI don’t want to sound like I’m just talking at people all the time, but… yes, I think that happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble following through on instructions or finishing tasks at home or in your free time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s the main problem for me, honestly. I mean… at home I do try to finish what I’m asked to do, and if my wife gives me something clear, I usually want to get it done.\n\nThere are probably times I don’t follow through as neatly as I should, but I don’t know if it’s frequent enough that I’d want to say “often.” I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Maybe I’d need an example of what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think I’d have to say yes, even though I don’t love admitting that.

At work, it’s gotten more serious than just people being annoyed with me. There have been complaints, and I was put on a corrective plan, which… honestly, I’m embarrassed to even say out loud. I do care about my job and I’m trying to be helpful, but I think the way I come across is hurting trust with colleagues.

And at home, my wife has said the caregiving routines are harder because people have to manage my energy too, on top of everything else. I don’t think she says it to be cruel. It’s just… I can see how I add another layer.

So yes, I guess it is affecting my quality of life and relationships in a significant way. I’m trying, but I’m worried I’m damaging trust with people I really care about.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I’m here because lately I feel like I’m just… always going. Like I finish one thing at school or at home and before I even take a breath, I’m already jumping into the next thing. It’s like I can’t quite shut myself off, if that makes sense. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s been hard to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think I do, though I don’t mean to be rude or anything. It’s more like I jump in because I think I’m helping. At school, with colleagues or parents, I’ll sort of take over the conversation before they’ve finished. And at home too, with my wife or relatives, I’ll start talking over them because I feel like I know where it’s going.\n\nI realize afterward that I didn’t really give them a chance to finish. I’m not trying to bulldoze people, but… yeah, I guess that is me acting before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to focus on one thing for a long time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I can focus when I need to, especially if it’s something important. Maybe I get a little restless with longer things, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s a major problem. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fidgety or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, more than I probably like to admit. I mean, I try to keep it contained, especially in professional settings, but in IEP meetings or my admin classes my leg will be bouncing under the table, or I’ll be clicking a pen, shifting around in the chair.\n\nSometimes I don’t even notice I’m doing it until someone glances over, and then I feel a little embarrassed. I’m not trying to be disruptive, obviously, but sitting still for long stretches is… hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have mood swings?\n\nPatient: Mood swings… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have big mood swings, at least not that I’ve noticed. I mean, I can get frustrated or keyed up like anyone, but I try not to take that out on people.\n\nMaybe my wife would have a better read on that than I do. But from my perspective, I don’t think it’s a major thing. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I mean… probably, yes. I don’t like to think of myself as dominating a room, but I can definitely talk more than I intend to.\n\nLike in a parent conference or a staff meeting, I’ll go in meaning to keep it short, and then I’ll hear myself giving three examples, or telling a classroom story, or offering ideas that… maybe nobody actually asked for. And then I’ll notice the other person kind of trying to wrap it up, and I feel bad. I’m not trying to be rude or anything. I just get going sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I try pretty hard to stay organized, especially with work, because I kind of have to.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m naturally organized, exactly, but I’ve built routines and lists and reminders so things don’t fall through the cracks. So… difficulty organizing? Maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than what most people deal with. Does that make sense, or are you asking more about planning steps and keeping track of materials?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I probably do, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI don’t mean to cut people off, but I’ll think I know where they’re going, or I’ll think I’m helping, and I jump in. It happens with colleagues, sometimes with parents at school, and definitely at home with my wife or relatives. I’ll start answering or sort of taking charge before they’ve even finished what they were saying.\n\nI usually realize it afterward—or when I see their face—and then I feel bad, because I’m not trying to be disrespectful. I just… jump in too fast sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often fidget or feel restless when you're alone or in quiet environments?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m not running around or anything, but even when things are quiet, I don’t seem to sit still very well.\n\nLike in IEP meetings or in my admin classes, I’ll realize my leg has been bouncing under the table the whole time, or I’m clicking a pen, or shifting around in the chair. Sometimes people glance over, and that’s when I notice I’m doing it. It’s a little embarrassing, honestly, because I’m not trying to be distracting. It just kind of happens before I realize it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often intrude on others or insert yourself into their activities without being asked?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, though I don’t like the word “intrude,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll jump into a conversation between colleagues, or with parents, or at home with my wife and a relative, because I think I’m being helpful or moving things along. But then I realize I’ve kind of talked over people or taken charge before they even finished saying what they needed to say.\n\nI’m not trying to be rude. I usually mean well. But yes, I can see how it comes across that way sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behaviors or tendencies are causing problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wish I could say it’s just a personality thing or that I’m just enthusiastic, but it is causing problems.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten more serious than I’m comfortable admitting. There have been enough complaints that I was put on a corrective plan, which is… embarrassing. I’m not trying to be difficult—I really do think I’m trying to help or keep things moving—but I can see that people don’t always experience it that way.\n\nAnd at home, my wife has said that caregiving routines are harder because everyone has to manage my energy too, on top of everything else. That’s hard to hear. I feel like I’m damaging trust with colleagues and with my family, even though my intention is usually to help. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms for most of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound like I was some terrible kid or anything, but this goes way back.\n\nMy mother used to joke that in elementary school I was “teaching the class from my seat,” because I’d be out of my chair, talking, or cutting in before the teacher had even finished giving directions. She said it with humor, but… looking back, it does sound like the same pattern.\n\nSo no, I don’t think this just started recently with the family situation or stress. It’s been part of me for a long time. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often dominate conversations with friends or family members outside of work or school?\n\nPatient: Yes… probably more than I realize. I don’t mean to dominate, exactly, but I can see that I do.\n\nEven outside of work, I’ll start out trying to make a quick point, and then suddenly I’m giving three examples, telling some related story, and offering ideas nobody really asked for. I can sometimes notice the other person is kind of trying to wrap it up, and I’m still going.\n\nI don’t want to sound like I’m just talking at people all the time, but… yes, I think that happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble following through on instructions or finishing tasks at home or in your free time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s the main problem for me, honestly. I mean… at home I do try to finish what I’m asked to do, and if my wife gives me something clear, I usually want to get it done.\n\nThere are probably times I don’t follow through as neatly as I should, but I don’t know if it’s frequent enough that I’d want to say “often.” I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Maybe I’d need an example of what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms are affecting your overall quality of life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I’d have to say yes, even though I don’t love admitting that.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten more serious than just people being annoyed with me. There have been complaints, and I was put on a corrective plan, which… honestly, I’m embarrassed to even say out loud. I do care about my job and I’m trying to be helpful, but I think the way I come across is hurting trust with colleagues.\n\nAnd at home, my wife has said the caregiving routines are harder because people have to manage my energy too, on top of everything else. I don’t think she says it to be cruel. It’s just… I can see how I add another layer.\n\nSo yes, I guess it is affecting my quality of life and relationships in a significant way. I’m trying, but I’m worried I’m damaging trust with people I really care about.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Maybe I’d need an example of what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms are affecting your overall quality of life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I’d have to say yes, even though I don’t love admitting that.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten more serious than just people being annoyed with me. There have been complaints, and I was put on a corrective plan, which… honestly, I’m embarrassed to even say out loud. I do care about my job and I’m trying to be helpful, but I think the way I come across is hurting trust with colleagues.\n\nAnd at home, my wife has said the caregiving routines are harder because people have to manage my energy too, on top of everything else. I don’t think she says it to be cruel. It’s just… I can see how I add another layer.\n\nSo yes, I guess it is affecting my quality of life and relationships in a significant way. I’m trying, but I’m worried I’m damaging trust with people I really care about.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced these symptoms consistently across different environments, such as at home, school, and work?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 58 / special_education_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"At work, the complaints have gotten serious enough that I was put on a corrective plan, and at home my spouse says caregiving routines are harder because people have to manage my energy on top of everything else. I feel like I’m damaging trust with colleagues and family even though I’m trying to help.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think it does show up in more than one place. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m the same level of problem everywhere, but it’s not just one setting.

At work, it’s been the most obvious, I guess. The complaints got serious enough that I was put on a corrective plan, which is hard to say because I really am trying to do a good job and help people.

And at home, my wife has told me the caregiving routines are harder because people have to manage my energy too, on top of everything else. So… yes, I think it’s across environments. I feel like I’m damaging trust with colleagues and with family, even though that’s the opposite of what I’m trying to do.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I’m here because lately I feel like I’m just… always going. Like I finish one thing at school or at home and before I even take a breath, I’m already jumping into the next thing. It’s like I can’t quite shut myself off, if that makes sense. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s been hard to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think I do, though I don’t mean to be rude or anything. It’s more like I jump in because I think I’m helping. At school, with colleagues or parents, I’ll sort of take over the conversation before they’ve finished. And at home too, with my wife or relatives, I’ll start talking over them because I feel like I know where it’s going.\n\nI realize afterward that I didn’t really give them a chance to finish. I’m not trying to bulldoze people, but… yeah, I guess that is me acting before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to focus on one thing for a long time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I can focus when I need to, especially if it’s something important. Maybe I get a little restless with longer things, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s a major problem. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fidgety or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, more than I probably like to admit. I mean, I try to keep it contained, especially in professional settings, but in IEP meetings or my admin classes my leg will be bouncing under the table, or I’ll be clicking a pen, shifting around in the chair.\n\nSometimes I don’t even notice I’m doing it until someone glances over, and then I feel a little embarrassed. I’m not trying to be disruptive, obviously, but sitting still for long stretches is… hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have mood swings?\n\nPatient: Mood swings… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have big mood swings, at least not that I’ve noticed. I mean, I can get frustrated or keyed up like anyone, but I try not to take that out on people.\n\nMaybe my wife would have a better read on that than I do. But from my perspective, I don’t think it’s a major thing. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I mean… probably, yes. I don’t like to think of myself as dominating a room, but I can definitely talk more than I intend to.\n\nLike in a parent conference or a staff meeting, I’ll go in meaning to keep it short, and then I’ll hear myself giving three examples, or telling a classroom story, or offering ideas that… maybe nobody actually asked for. And then I’ll notice the other person kind of trying to wrap it up, and I feel bad. I’m not trying to be rude or anything. I just get going sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I try pretty hard to stay organized, especially with work, because I kind of have to.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m naturally organized, exactly, but I’ve built routines and lists and reminders so things don’t fall through the cracks. So… difficulty organizing? Maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than what most people deal with. Does that make sense, or are you asking more about planning steps and keeping track of materials?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I probably do, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI don’t mean to cut people off, but I’ll think I know where they’re going, or I’ll think I’m helping, and I jump in. It happens with colleagues, sometimes with parents at school, and definitely at home with my wife or relatives. I’ll start answering or sort of taking charge before they’ve even finished what they were saying.\n\nI usually realize it afterward—or when I see their face—and then I feel bad, because I’m not trying to be disrespectful. I just… jump in too fast sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often fidget or feel restless when you're alone or in quiet environments?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m not running around or anything, but even when things are quiet, I don’t seem to sit still very well.\n\nLike in IEP meetings or in my admin classes, I’ll realize my leg has been bouncing under the table the whole time, or I’m clicking a pen, or shifting around in the chair. Sometimes people glance over, and that’s when I notice I’m doing it. It’s a little embarrassing, honestly, because I’m not trying to be distracting. It just kind of happens before I realize it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often intrude on others or insert yourself into their activities without being asked?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, though I don’t like the word “intrude,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll jump into a conversation between colleagues, or with parents, or at home with my wife and a relative, because I think I’m being helpful or moving things along. But then I realize I’ve kind of talked over people or taken charge before they even finished saying what they needed to say.\n\nI’m not trying to be rude. I usually mean well. But yes, I can see how it comes across that way sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behaviors or tendencies are causing problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wish I could say it’s just a personality thing or that I’m just enthusiastic, but it is causing problems.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten more serious than I’m comfortable admitting. There have been enough complaints that I was put on a corrective plan, which is… embarrassing. I’m not trying to be difficult—I really do think I’m trying to help or keep things moving—but I can see that people don’t always experience it that way.\n\nAnd at home, my wife has said that caregiving routines are harder because everyone has to manage my energy too, on top of everything else. That’s hard to hear. I feel like I’m damaging trust with colleagues and with my family, even though my intention is usually to help. I don’t want to make it sound like I was some terrible kid or anything, but this goes way back.\n\nMy mother used to joke that in elementary school I was “teaching the class from my seat,” because I’d be out of my chair, talking, or cutting in before the teacher had even finished giving directions. She said it with humor, but… looking back, it does sound like the same pattern.\n\nSo no, I don’t think this just started recently with the family situation or stress. It’s been part of me for a long time. I feel like I’m damaging trust with colleagues and family even though I’m trying to help.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 8 months\n  manifestation: This level of trouble has been going on for about 8 months, since our older relative moved in with us.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 58 / special_education_teacher / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S014\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble following through on instructions or finishing tasks at home or in your free time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s the main problem for me, honestly. I mean… at home I do try to finish what I’m asked to do, and if my wife gives me something clear, I usually want to get it done.\n\nThere are probably times I don’t follow through as neatly as I should, but I don’t know if it’s frequent enough that I’d want to say “often.” I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Maybe I’d need an example of what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms are affecting your overall quality of life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I’d have to say yes, even though I don’t love admitting that.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten more serious than just people being annoyed with me. There have been complaints, and I was put on a corrective plan, which… honestly, I’m embarrassed to even say out loud. I do care about my job and I’m trying to be helpful, but I think the way I come across is hurting trust with colleagues.\n\nAnd at home, my wife has said the caregiving routines are harder because people have to manage my energy too, on top of everything else. I don’t think she says it to be cruel. It’s just… I can see how I add another layer.\n\nSo yes, I guess it is affecting my quality of life and relationships in a significant way. I’m trying, but I’m worried I’m damaging trust with people I really care about.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms consistently across different environments, such as at home, school, and work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does show up in more than one place. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m the same level of problem everywhere, but it’s not just one setting.\n\nAt work, it’s been the most obvious, I guess. The complaints got serious enough that I was put on a corrective plan, which is hard to say because I really am trying to do a good job and help people.\n\nAnd at home, my wife has told me the caregiving routines are harder because people have to manage my energy too, on top of everything else. So… yes, I think it’s across environments. I feel like I’m damaging trust with colleagues and with family, even though that’s the opposite of what I’m trying to do.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid you exhibit these behaviors as a child, or did they develop later in life?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. This was part of who I was long before adulthood, not something that just started with this family change.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it was there when I was a kid, yes. I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible child or anything, but… my mother used to joke that even in elementary school I sort of “taught the class from my seat.”

She meant I was always out of my chair, talking, or jumping in before the teacher even finished giving directions. At the time it was kind of treated as me being energetic or maybe funny, but looking back, it does seem like this was part of who I was long before adulthood. It’s not something that just started with the recent family changes.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I’m here because lately I feel like I’m just… always going. Like I finish one thing at school or at home and before I even take a breath, I’m already jumping into the next thing. It’s like I can’t quite shut myself off, if that makes sense. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s been hard to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think I do, though I don’t mean to be rude or anything. It’s more like I jump in because I think I’m helping. At school, with colleagues or parents, I’ll sort of take over the conversation before they’ve finished. And at home too, with my wife or relatives, I’ll start talking over them because I feel like I know where it’s going.\n\nI realize afterward that I didn’t really give them a chance to finish. I’m not trying to bulldoze people, but… yeah, I guess that is me acting before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to focus on one thing for a long time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I can focus when I need to, especially if it’s something important. Maybe I get a little restless with longer things, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s a major problem. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel fidgety or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, more than I probably like to admit. I mean, I try to keep it contained, especially in professional settings, but in IEP meetings or my admin classes my leg will be bouncing under the table, or I’ll be clicking a pen, shifting around in the chair.\n\nSometimes I don’t even notice I’m doing it until someone glances over, and then I feel a little embarrassed. I’m not trying to be disruptive, obviously, but sitting still for long stretches is… hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have mood swings?\n\nPatient: Mood swings… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have big mood swings, at least not that I’ve noticed. I mean, I can get frustrated or keyed up like anyone, but I try not to take that out on people.\n\nMaybe my wife would have a better read on that than I do. But from my perspective, I don’t think it’s a major thing. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I mean… probably, yes. I don’t like to think of myself as dominating a room, but I can definitely talk more than I intend to.\n\nLike in a parent conference or a staff meeting, I’ll go in meaning to keep it short, and then I’ll hear myself giving three examples, or telling a classroom story, or offering ideas that… maybe nobody actually asked for. And then I’ll notice the other person kind of trying to wrap it up, and I feel bad. I’m not trying to be rude or anything. I just get going sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I try pretty hard to stay organized, especially with work, because I kind of have to.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m naturally organized, exactly, but I’ve built routines and lists and reminders so things don’t fall through the cracks. So… difficulty organizing? Maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s more than what most people deal with. Does that make sense, or are you asking more about planning steps and keeping track of materials?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I probably do, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI don’t mean to cut people off, but I’ll think I know where they’re going, or I’ll think I’m helping, and I jump in. It happens with colleagues, sometimes with parents at school, and definitely at home with my wife or relatives. I’ll start answering or sort of taking charge before they’ve even finished what they were saying.\n\nI usually realize it afterward—or when I see their face—and then I feel bad, because I’m not trying to be disrespectful. I just… jump in too fast sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often fidget or feel restless when you're alone or in quiet environments?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m not running around or anything, but even when things are quiet, I don’t seem to sit still very well.\n\nLike in IEP meetings or in my admin classes, I’ll realize my leg has been bouncing under the table the whole time, or I’m clicking a pen, or shifting around in the chair. Sometimes people glance over, and that’s when I notice I’m doing it. It’s a little embarrassing, honestly, because I’m not trying to be distracting. It just kind of happens before I realize it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often intrude on others or insert yourself into their activities without being asked?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, though I don’t like the word “intrude,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll jump into a conversation between colleagues, or with parents, or at home with my wife and a relative, because I think I’m being helpful or moving things along. But then I realize I’ve kind of talked over people or taken charge before they even finished saying what they needed to say.\n\nI’m not trying to be rude. I usually mean well. But yes, I can see how it comes across that way sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behaviors or tendencies are causing problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wish I could say it’s just a personality thing or that I’m just enthusiastic, but it is causing problems.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten more serious than I’m comfortable admitting. There have been enough complaints that I was put on a corrective plan, which is… embarrassing. I’m not trying to be difficult—I really do think I’m trying to help or keep things moving—but I can see that people don’t always experience it that way.\n\nAnd at home, my wife has said that caregiving routines are harder because everyone has to manage my energy too, on top of everything else. That’s hard to hear. I feel like I’m damaging trust with colleagues and with my family, even though my intention is usually to help. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms for most of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound like I was some terrible kid or anything, but this goes way back.\n\nMy mother used to joke that in elementary school I was “teaching the class from my seat,” because I’d be out of my chair, talking, or cutting in before the teacher had even finished giving directions. She said it with humor, but… looking back, it does sound like the same pattern.\n\nSo no, I don’t think this just started recently with the family situation or stress. It’s been part of me for a long time. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often dominate conversations with friends or family members outside of work or school?\n\nPatient: Yes… probably more than I realize. I don’t mean to dominate, exactly, but I can see that I do.\n\nEven outside of work, I’ll start out trying to make a quick point, and then suddenly I’m giving three examples, telling some related story, and offering ideas nobody really asked for. I can sometimes notice the other person is kind of trying to wrap it up, and I’m still going.\n\nI don’t want to sound like I’m just talking at people all the time, but… yes, I think that happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble following through on instructions or finishing tasks at home or in your free time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s the main problem for me, honestly. I mean… at home I do try to finish what I’m asked to do, and if my wife gives me something clear, I usually want to get it done.\n\nThere are probably times I don’t follow through as neatly as I should, but I don’t know if it’s frequent enough that I’d want to say “often.” I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Maybe I’d need an example of what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms are affecting your overall quality of life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I’d have to say yes, even though I don’t love admitting that.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten more serious than just people being annoyed with me. There have been complaints, and I was put on a corrective plan, which… honestly, I’m embarrassed to even say out loud. I do care about my job and I’m trying to be helpful, but I think the way I come across is hurting trust with colleagues.\n\nAnd at home, my wife has said the caregiving routines are harder because people have to manage my energy too, on top of everything else. I don’t think she says it to be cruel. It’s just… I can see how I add another layer.\n\nSo yes, I guess it is affecting my quality of life and relationships in a significant way. I’m trying, but I’m worried I’m damaging trust with people I really care about.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms consistently across different environments, such as at home, school, and work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does show up in more than one place. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m the same level of problem everywhere, but it’s not just one setting.\n\nAt work, it’s been the most obvious, I guess. The complaints got serious enough that I was put on a corrective plan, which is hard to say because I really am trying to do a good job and help people.\n\nAnd at home, my wife has told me the caregiving routines are harder because people have to manage my energy too, on top of everything else. So… yes, I think it’s across environments. I feel like I’m damaging trust with colleagues and with family, even though that’s the opposite of what I’m trying to do.\n\nDoctor: Did you exhibit these behaviors as a child, or did they develop later in life?\n\nPatient: I think it was there when I was a kid, yes. I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible child or anything, but… my mother used to joke that even in elementary school I sort of “taught the class from my seat.”\n\nShe meant I was always out of my chair, talking, or jumping in before the teacher even finished giving directions. At the time it was kind of treated as me being energetic or maybe funny, but looking back, it does seem like this was part of who I was long before adulthood. It’s not something that just started with the recent family changes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
